─── apps/quantize/parameter_reader.h:22-23 ───

─── apps/perplexity/corpus.h:24-24 ───
[maintainability · low] The `bool quick` parameter is a "boolean trap": at the call site (e.g.
`load_corpus(*options.corpus, options.quick)`) the meaning of the argument is not self-documenting,
and `load_corpus(manifest, true/false)` is easy to misread. Consider exposing the selection mode as
a named enum (e.g. `enum class CorpusMode { Quick, Full }`) so callers cannot accidentally pass the

─── apps/perplexity/corpus.h:25-25 ───
[documentation · low] Both API functions signal all failures by throwing `std::runtime_error`
(unreadable/missing file, invalid JSON, non-UTF-8/NUL-containing text, path traversal / unknown or
duplicate stream ids), but the header documents none of this. Consumers of this public header have
no contract to rely on. Add a brief doc comment stating the throwing behavior and preconditions
(manifest must exist and be well-formed; text files must be non-empty NUL-free UTF-8).



─── apps/quantize/calibration.h:20-20 ───
[maintainability · medium] `skipped_names` is documented as the count of eligible names with no
observed site, but the sole implementation of `calibrate()` unconditionally sets it to `0`
(apps/quantize/calibration.cpp:423). As a result the header advertises a meaningful result that is
never produced, and consumers such as `apps/quantize/main.cpp:230` silently report 0 for every run.
Either populate this field when names are dropped (e.g. MTP layers that are never observed), or
remove it so the API does not promise semantics it does not deliver.



─── apps/quantize/source_writer.h:27-27 ───
[bug · medium] `states` is a bare pointer with no length, yet the implementation unconditionally
wraps it as `std::span<const std::uint16_t>(states, kExl3WeightsPerTile)` (256 elements) and reads
all of them. A caller that passes a shorter buffer or a null pointer gets an out-of-bounds read with
no way for the callee to detect it. Encode the fixed 256-element contract in the type (e.g.
`std::span<const std::uint16_t, 256>` or a `std::array` reference) so the size is checked at the
call site; add `#include <span>` accordingly.

- [[nodiscard]] std::vector<std::byte> pack_trellis_states(const std::uint16_t* states,
+ [[nodiscard]] std::vector<std::byte> pack_trellis_states(std::span<const std::uint16_t, 256> states,


─── apps/quantize/source_writer.h:28-28 ───
[bug · low] `bitrate_half_bits` is declared as a plain `int` with no documented or enforced range.
The implementation casts it to `std::uint64_t`, so a negative or otherwise out-of-range value
silently wraps and can lead to an oversized/invalid byte stream. Document the valid range (and/or
take an unsigned type) so callers cannot pass an invalid bitrate unknowingly.



─── apps/quantize/hessian_io.cpp:9-9 ───
[bug · medium] `k * k` is computed in `std::uint64_t` with no upper bound on `k`. For a large `k`
(e.g. a malformed/hostile model metadata) this multiplication (and the later `count *
sizeof(float)`) can silently wrap around, producing an undersized buffer and a size check against a
wrapped value, so the short-file guard no longer protects the read. Consider validating `k` (and the
product) before allocating, e.g. reject when `k != 0 && k >
std::numeric_limits<std::uint64_t>::max() / k`.



─── apps/quantize/hessian_io.cpp:36-36 ───
[bug · medium] Same unbounded `k * k` computation as `read_hessian_f32`: for a large `k` the product
wraps (and on a 32-bit `std::size_t` the cast truncates), yielding a silently undersized identity
matrix and out-of-bounds indexing in the loop below. Validate `k` before the multiplication.



─── apps/perplexity/evaluation.h:42-43 ───
[maintainability · medium] These two entry points take a raw pointer plus a separately passed
`vocab` length. Nothing ties the buffer to the length, so a caller can easily pass a mismatched pair
and cause an out-of-bounds read (the implementation indexes `row[v]` for `v < vocab` with no bound
in the type). Other APIs in this header already use `std::span` (see `ScoreAggregate::add`). Prefer
`std::span<const std::uint16_t>` here too, deriving the row length from the span and removing the
redundant `vocab` parameter.



─── apps/perplexity/evaluation.h:15-15 ───
[maintainability · low] `first_target` is not an absolute sequence index like the other fields: it
is a window-local offset (it is added to the input offset when scoring). The name/semantics are easy
to confuse with `target_begin`. Consider renaming (e.g. `local_target_begin`/`scored_offset`) or
documenting it so callers do not treat it as a token index.



─── apps/perplexity/evaluation.h:26-26 ───
[bug · medium] This merge overload is declared `noexcept`, while the `std::span` overload is allowed
to throw on `scored_tokens` overflow. Merging two aggregates therefore silently wraps
`scored_tokens` (and produce a wrong `mean_nll`/`ppl`) once the combined count exceeds `uint64_t`.
Either validate the accumulation on merge (and drop `noexcept`) or document the saturation behaviour
explicitly so the inconsistency with `add(std::span)` is intentional.



─── apps/serve/main.cpp:51-54 ───
[bug · high] The signal handler invokes `server->stop()`, which resolves to `HttpServer::stop()` →
`httplib::Server::stop()`. That path takes internal mutexes and closes sockets, none of which are
async-signal-safe. If a signal is delivered on a thread that currently holds one of those locks
(e.g. while `listen()`/route handling is running), the handler can deadlock or corrupt state — the
POSIX contract only permits async-signal-safe functions here. Prefer setting an `std::atomic<bool>`
flag in the handler and having the main/accept loop poll it to perform the stop (or use a
self-pipe), and install the handler with `sigaction` rather than `std::signal`.



─── apps/serve/main.cpp:81-81 ───
[bug · medium] `g_logging` is set to point at the stack-local `logging` object and is never reset,
unlike `g_server` which is cleared to `nullptr`. The terminate handler is global and can fire at any
time, including after `main` returns (during static destruction) or from a worker thread once
`logging` has gone out of scope, at which point `log_terminate` dereferences a dangling pointer and
flushes a destroyed logger (UB). Clear the pointer when the owning scope ends, or ensure the object
outlives any possible `terminate` invocation.



─── apps/quantize/trace_reader.h:17-17 ───
[bug · low] `lengths` (and `input_ids`) are declared `std::int32_t`, but `row_tokens` is
`std::uint32_t` and the reader only checks `length <= row_tokens` before narrowing `int64 -> int32`
(see `trace_reader.cpp`). A trace whose row width exceeds `INT32_MAX` (yet still <= `UINT32_MAX`)
would make the reader's narrowing cast overflow into a negative value. Make the element types
consistent with the dimension types (e.g. `std::uint32_t`), or explicitly document/cap the I32
domain in the contract.



─── apps/quantize/trace_reader.h:16-16 ───
[documentation · low] The file-level comment states the on-disk tensors are `I64`, but the in-memory
fields are `int32_t`/`uint32_t`. The narrowing (and the I32 token-id restriction enforced by the
reader) is not described on the struct, so callers may wrongly assume 64-bit values. Consider noting
the I32 in-memory representation here.



─── apps/quantize/trace_reader.cpp:104-109 ───
[security · critical] `read_i64` computes `count` from the declared tensor shape but never checks it
against the entry's declared data range `[begin, end)`. `parse_header` only guarantees `end <=
data_bytes`, not that `count * sizeof(int64_t) <= end - begin`. If a header declares a shape larger
than its `data_offsets` range (malformed or malicious trace), the `memcpy` reads past the entry's
region and potentially past the end of `bytes`, causing a heap out-of-bounds read. Add a bounds
check before copying (using division to avoid overflow).

      const auto read_i64 = [&](const Entry& entry, std::uint64_t count) {
+         const std::uint64_t range = entry.end - entry.begin;
+         if (range < sizeof(std::int64_t) || count > range / sizeof(std::int64_t)) {
+             throw std::invalid_argument("trace tensor shape does not match its data range");
+         }
          std::vector<std::int64_t> out(static_cast<std::size_t>(count));
          std::memcpy(out.data(), bytes.data() + data_start + entry.begin,
                      static_cast<std::size_t>(count) * sizeof(std::int64_t));
          return out;
      };


─── apps/quantize/trace_reader.cpp:65-66 ───
[maintainability · medium] `require` is defined in the anonymous namespace but is never called
anywhere in the translation unit (verified: no call sites). It is dead code and will also trigger an
unused-function warning. Remove it, or use it in place of the ad-hoc tensor selection logic below.
Note it also contains a stray trailing space in the message: `"expected I64[" + expected + " ]"`.



─── apps/quantize/trace_reader.cpp:89-91 ───
[bug · medium] Tensor selection is heuristic and ignores tensor names entirely: it picks whichever
2-D I64 tensor has the largest last dimension and overwrites `lengths` with every 1-D I64 tensor
encountered (last one wins). If the file contains other I64 tensors (e.g. an attention mask with a
wider row, or a second 1-D I64 tensor), the wrong tensors can be chosen; the only safeguard is the
`rows`-match check, which is not sufficient when dimensions coincide. Consider matching by the
safetensors entry name (e.g. `input_ids`/`lengths`) rather than by shape heuristics.



─── apps/quantize/source_writer.cpp:20-20 ───
[bug · medium] Duplicate tensor names are not detected. `header[tensor.name] = ...` silently
overwrites the previous entry, but `data.insert(...)` still appends the duplicate tensor's bytes.
The result is a file whose JSON header describes only one entry while the data buffer contains both,
so the offsets no longer match the payload (corrupt/unreadable safetensors). Consider rejecting
duplicates explicitly.

          if (tensor.name.empty()) { throw std::invalid_argument("tensor name must not be empty"); }
+         if (header.contains(tensor.name)) {
+             throw std::invalid_argument("duplicate tensor name: " + tensor.name);
+         }


─── apps/quantize/source_writer.cpp:28-28 ───
[performance · medium] Every tensor's bytes are first copied into the intermediate `data` vector and
then written out. For a quantized model shard this can be hundreds of MB/GB, doubling peak memory
and adding a full extra copy. Since the header only needs the offsets (already computed from
`tensor.data.size()`), you can emit the header first and then stream each `tensor.data` directly to
`out`, avoiding the accumulation entirely.



─── apps/quantize/source_writer.cpp:37-37 ───
[bug · low] The header length is written in the host's native byte order. The safetensors format
mandates a little-endian 64-bit length, so on a big-endian target this produces an unreadable file.
Consider serializing the value explicitly to little-endian bytes rather than `reinterpret_cast`-ing
the native `uint64_t`.



─── apps/quantize/options.cpp:169-171 ───

unconditionally. So `ninfer-quantize model.ninfer --out DIR --hessians DIR --calibrate-only` (no
`--trace`) returns success (exit 0) after only creating the output directory and writing no Hessians
at all — a silent no-op that looks like a successful run. Validation should reject this combination.

      if (!options.trace.empty() && options.activation_model.empty()) {

─── apps/quantize/options.cpp:26-26 ───

─── apps/quantize/options.cpp:6-6 ───
[maintainability · low] `<vector>` is included but never used in this translation unit; it can be

─── apps/quantize/options.cpp:75-75 ───
[maintainability · low] `sanitize_parameter_name` maps `/`, `\` and `:` to `_`, which is not
injective: distinct logical names such as `a/b` and `a_b` collapse to the same string. Since the
sanitized name keys one Hessian file per parameter (see calibration.cpp/main.cpp), a collision
silently overwrites the other parameter's Hessian. Consider documenting this assumption or
detecting/handling collisions.



─── apps/quantize/options.h:52-52 ───
[bug · medium] `sanitize_parameter_name` is a lossy mapping (it rewrites '/', '\' and ':' to '_',
not just '/'), so distinct logical names can collapse to the same flat string (e.g. "a/b_c" and
"a_b/c" both become "a_b_c"). Callers use the result as the per-parameter Hessian file name and as
the output safetensors stem, so such a collision would silently overwrite another parameter's
Hessian/data instead of failing. Consider detecting/guarding collisions (e.g. appending a short hash
or asserting uniqueness) and updating this contract comment to document the full replacement set and
the empty-name exception actually thrown by the implementation.



─── apps/quantize/options.h:57-57 ───
[documentation · low] The contract says "the output head uses head_bits_half", but artifacts expose
several output heads (e.g. `text/output_head`, `mtp/output_head`, and the draft/proposal head). The
implementation only special-cases the literal `text/output_head`, so the other heads silently fall
back to `--bits`. Please state explicitly which heads this applies to (or make the doc generic) so
callers are not misled.



─── apps/perplexity/reference.h:19-19 ───
[documentation · medium] This comment states "Loading validates every field, so a text or protocol
mismatch is refused instead of silently mis-aligning rows," but that is not what `load()` does: it
only *stores* the 64-byte digest (`text_digest_`) without comparing it to any expected text, and it
never validates `context_`/`stride_` (nor `reserved`). The context/stride check and the "no
positions matched" error live in the caller (main.cpp), not in loading. A reference built from a
*different* text that happens to share some scored global positions would therefore be silently
consumed by `row()` (no digest check, no misalignment error), producing wrong KL values. Either make
the guarantee real (e.g. pass the expected digest to `load()` and reject on mismatch, and validate
context/stride) or correct this comment so callers know they must compare `text_digest()`
themselves.



─── apps/perplexity/reference.h:39-39 ───
[maintainability · low] `row()` hands back a bare pointer whose valid length (`vocab_size()`
entries) is implicit, requiring callers to remember the row length and to null-check. Returning
`std::span<const std::uint16_t>` (e.g. via `std::optional<std::span<const std::uint16_t>>` for the
"not scored" case) would make the row extent explicit, prevent out-of-bounds reads, and remove the
raw-pointer/nullptr sentinel.



─── apps/cli/options.h:29-29 ───
[maintainability · low] The default `kv_capacity` hardcodes the same literal (`2048`) as

`parse_options` always overwrites this default when `--kv-capacity` is not given
(`options.kv_capacity = KvCapacityPolicy::explicit_capacity(options.max_context)`), so the header
default only matters for callers that construct `Options` directly. If `max_context` is ever raised
here without also updating this line, those callers would produce an invalid combination
(`explicit_tokens < max_context`) that Engine/`parse_options` rejects. Consider deriving the default
from a single named constant.

─── apps/perplexity/reference.cpp:64-65 ───
[bug · medium] `load()` only overwrites `positions_`/`logits_` but never resets `index_`, so
re-loading on an already-populated `Reference` reuses stale entries. If the same file is loaded
twice, `index_.emplace` hits the pre-existing keys and throws a spurious "duplicate scored
position"; if a smaller reference is loaded after a larger one, stale indices from the previous load
can exceed the new `logits_` size and make `row()` read out of bounds. Clear the member state at the
start of `load()` (or build into locals and swap).

+     positions_.clear();
+     logits_.clear();
+     index_.clear();
      index_.reserve(rows);
      for (std::uint32_t row = 0; row < rows; ++row) {


─── apps/perplexity/reference.cpp:114-115 ───
[bug · low] The reader decodes the position table as explicit little-endian (`le_u32`) and the
documented layout is "little-endian throughout", but the writer serializes `positions` (and
`logits`) in the host's native byte order. On a big-endian host the file written here would be
misread by `load()` (and is asymmetric with the reader, which byte-swaps positions but not logits).
Serialize the fields explicitly little-endian to match the reader/format contract.



─── apps/quantize/parameter_reader.cpp:114-116 ───
[security · medium] The bounds check multiplies two 64-bit values before comparing, so it can
overflow. With a malformed artifact where `part.end` is very large, `part.end * bytes_per_word`
wraps around and the guard passes even though the range is out of bounds, after which
`payload[at]`/`payload[at + 1]` read past the vector. Compare using division so the multiplication
cannot wrap.

-         if (part.end * bytes_per_word > payload.size()) {
+         if (part.end > payload.size() / bytes_per_word) {
              throw std::runtime_error(parameter.name + ": binding range exceeds its object");
          }


─── apps/quantize/parameter_reader.cpp:74-74 ───
[maintainability · low] `members` is only ever populated via `members[...].push_back(...)`, so every
value vector is guaranteed to contain at least one element. This `list.empty()` branch is therefore
unreachable dead code and can be removed.



─── apps/quantize/parameter_reader.cpp:86-86 ───
[maintainability · low] `members` is built only from bindings that already passed
`binding.parts.size() != 1` above, and `bindings.at(name)` returns the same binding, so the
`binding.parts.size() != 1` disjunct can never be true here. Dropping it makes the actual contiguity

─── apps/cli/main.cpp:172-173 ───
[bug · medium] `prefill_seconds` measures only the tokens actually processed by prefill, but the
numerator here is the full prompt token count. When prefix/cache reuse occurs the reused tokens are
skipped, so the elapsed time shrinks while the numerator stays the same, overstating the reported
prefill throughput. The engine computes processed prefill work as `prompt_tokens -
reused_prompt_tokens` (see `engine_core.h` and the assertions in
`tests/models/qwen3_5/test_engine_prefix_real.cpp`), and the CLI already prints `reused prompt
tokens` separately. Use the prefilled token count as the numerator.

-     print_metric("prefill speed", format_rate(static_cast<double>(result.prompt.prompt_tokens),

+         static_cast<std::uint64_t>(result.prompt.prompt_tokens) - result.reused_prompt_tokens;
+     print_metric("prefill speed", format_rate(static_cast<double>(prefilled_tokens),
                                                result.timings.prefill_seconds));


─── apps/cli/main.cpp:252-257 ───

─── apps/perplexity/main.cpp:250-251 ───
[bug · medium] The reference's `text_digest()` is logged but never validated against the text this
run actually scores. The later `overall_kl.rows == 0` check ("...text or protocol mismatch") only
catches positions that do not overlap at all; a reference built from a *different* text of similar
length has the same positions, so its rows match and KL/NLL are silently computed against the wrong
distribution. The writer side already produces the digest via
`sha256_hex(sha256(streams.front().source.text))`, so after the corpus is loaded compare it to
`reference.text_digest()` (and fail, or restrict `--reference` to the single `--text` stream)
instead of relying on the row-overlap check.



─── apps/perplexity/main.cpp:479-480 ───
[bug · medium] `delta_nats` subtracts two means computed over different position sets: `overall`
aggregates every scored position of the run, while `overall_reference` only aggregates the positions
the reference actually covers (which is a strict subset when the reference was produced with
`--sample-stride > 1`). The result is a biased delta (and the accompanying comment already notes the
counts differ). Track the model's NLL restricted to the reference-scored positions (e.g. accumulate
a separate `ScoreAggregate` inside the reference sink) and compute `delta_nats` from that, so both
means cover identical positions.



─── apps/perplexity/main.cpp:503-504 ───
[bug · low] `prefill_chunk_tokens` and `score_tile_tokens` are hardcoded to 1024, but the engine
default is 2048/4096 in some configurations (see `EngineOptions::prefill_chunk`, "Empty selects the
model's default: 4096 for a model whose routed experts are host-resident ... and 1024 otherwise").
The report therefore states execution facts that may not match the run. Derive these from the
resolved engine options (e.g. `engine.options()`) or the load summary instead of literals.



─── apps/perplexity/main.cpp:148-148 ───
[maintainability · low] `--sample-stride` is only meaningful together with `--write-reference`, but
when passed without it the value is silently accepted and ignored. Given the other argument
validations here, reject the combination explicitly so a typo/misuse does not produce a report that
looks like sub-sampling was applied.



─── apps/perplexity/evaluation.cpp:50-50 ───
[bug · low] Exception-safety: the loop mutates `total_nll` before the `scored_tokens` capacity check
runs, so if the overflow `throw` fires the aggregate is left inconsistent (`total_nll` includes the
new logprobs but `scored_tokens` does not, making `mean_nll()`/`ppl()` wrong). Validate
`logprobs.size()` against the remaining `uint64` capacity *before* accumulating, so the operation
either fully succeeds or leaves the object untouched.



─── apps/perplexity/evaluation.cpp:70-73 ───
[bug · low] Unlike `ScoreAggregate::add`, this path never validates that `kl` is finite and is
`noexcept`, so a single NaN/inf value silently poisons `total_kl` forever while `rows` still
increments; `mean_kl()` then reports NaN with no diagnostic. This is reachable because
`kl_divergence_row` itself can return NaN (e.g. a degenerate row). Consider rejecting non-finite KL
values (or documenting that callers must). Note the merge overload has the same issue: `rows +=
other.rows` can silently wrap `uint64`.



─── apps/perplexity/evaluation.cpp:102-102 ───
[bug · low] Degenerate-row handling: if the whole row is `-inf` then `maximum` is `-inf` and
`bf16_to_float(row[v]) - maximum` evaluates to `-inf - (-inf) = NaN`, so `sum`/the return value
become NaN. A `NaN` logit leads to the same silent NaN result. Since this feeds
`kl_divergence_row`/`reference_logprob_row`, guard the all-`-inf`/non-finite case (or document the
precondition) instead of propagating NaN to the caller.

─── apps/sensitivity/main.cpp:179-179 ───
[bug · low] The stream state is only checked after opening; the actual write is never verified. On a

─── apps/quantize/main.cpp:310-311 ───
[bug · medium] The resume path trusts the sidecar blindly: the stream is never checked and

handler returns 1 before the index/report are written), discarding all previously completed
progress. A corrupt sidecar should instead cause that shard to be re-quantized. Also note

+                                      sidecar.string().c_str(), error.what());
+                     }
+                     if (!prior.is_object() || !prior.contains("names") ||
+                         !prior.contains("tensors")) {
+                         // fall through and re-quantize this bank

─── apps/quantize/calibration.cpp:348-351 ───
[bug · medium] The `expert_activation_observer()` / `expert_activation_wanted()` globals capture

dangling references to the destroyed `HessianGroup`, and the next forward pass in the same process
calls into freed memory. `calibration_observer()` is already protected by `ObserverScope`; the
expert observers need the same RAII treatment (install + reset in one guard destructor) instead of
manual assignment/reset.

─── apps/cli/options.cpp:319-320 ───
[bug · medium] `--profile` is documented (and relied upon in the comment above the arg split) as
never overriding the command line. That holds for scalar flags because a later assignment wins, but
`--prompt` and `--messages` are two independent fields: if the profile supplies `messages` and the
user supplies `--prompt` (or vice versa), both end up set and the run fails with the generic "pass
exactly one of --prompt or --messages", even though the user only passed one on the command line.
Consider tracking which of the two came from the CLI and clearing the profile-supplied counterpart
so "the command line always wins" actually holds.



─── apps/cli/options.cpp:109-109 ───

─── include/ninfer/ops/gemma_moe_experts.h:63-64 ───
[documentation · medium] The public workspace contract omits the buffer's alignment requirement. The
launcher's preconditions declare the workspace must be 256-byte aligned (see

buffer can silently reach kernels whose preconditions assume 256 bytes. Document the required
alignment here and make it consistent with the wrapper's validation and the kernel's assumption (16

─── include/ninfer/ops/gemma_moe_experts.h:86-86 ───

─── include/ninfer/ops/prepare_ragged_prefix.h:24-24 ───

─── include/ninfer/ops/rmsnorm_rope.h:67-67 ───
[bug · medium] The fused text Op bakes the RoPE base (1e7) and the RMS epsilon (1e-6) into its
contract: unlike `rope()`, which takes `theta`, and `rmsnorm()`, which takes `eps`, none of these
three overloads accept them. The doc above asserts the text form is bit-identical to `rmsnorm(q_in)
-> rmsnorm(k_in) -> rope(q_out, k_out)`, but that equivalence holds **only** when the caller's
configuration uses rope_theta == 1e7 and eps == 1e-6; nothing in the signature (and no stated
precondition) enforces this. A caller that selects this Op for a model with a different theta/eps
silently gets numerically different output — the equivalence claim is then false. Suggest either
taking theta/eps as parameters (consistent with `rope()`/`rmsnorm()`) or documenting the required
constants as an explicit precondition the dispatcher must verify.



─── include/ninfer/ops/rmsnorm_rope.h:53-53 ───
[documentation · low] Internal inconsistency in this block: the formula defines `n` purely in real
arithmetic (`n[d] = x[d] * inv * (norm_weight[d] + 1)`) and writes the rotation in terms of that
`n`, yet the following paragraph states the rotation consumes the **BF16-rounded** `n`. A reader
implementing the independent FP64 oracle from this formula will not reproduce the kernel for the
rotating channels. The in-place forms above explicitly forbid any BF16 materialization of `n`, so
this block alone is ambiguous — please make the BF16 rounding explicit in the formula (e.g. `nb[d] =
bf16(n[d])` and use `nb` in the rotation lines), as the `out[d] = n[d]` line for d >= 64 already
implies.

─── include/ninfer/ops/gemma_moe_router.h:39-39 ───
[documentation · medium] The output layout described here does not match what the op actually
writes. The selection kernel stores `ids[rank * tokens + token]` (and `weights[rank * tokens +
token]`), i.e. the *token* index is contiguous and the rank is the outer dimension — the same form
`gemma_moe_experts` reads back. So `(rank fastest axis)` is wrong: rank varies with stride `tokens`,
token varies with stride 1. This also clashes with the `H`-contiguous convention used for `x` a few
lines above (there, `[H,T]` really does mean hidden is the inline axis). With this project's
`Tensor` convention (ne[0] is the inline axis, see `set_contiguous_strides`), a consumer that
indexes an output `Tensor` shaped `{K,T}` by its own strides — or that trusts this annotation — will
read the wrong element. Please state the real layout (e.g. `[T,K], token fastest`) or make the
outputs rank-contiguous and align the peer op.



─── include/ninfer/ops/gemma_moe_router.h:85-85 ───

an undersized or unaligned workspace. It is also worth stating that `T` is not a parameter — it is
taken from `x`'s token extent — since callers must size the workspace and outputs from that same
value.

─── include/ninfer/ops/weight_input.h:16-16 ───

sentinel here.

─── src/artifact/file_io.h:28-28 ───
[bug · medium] `direct_fd_` is a lazily-opened resource mutated from the `const` `read_direct()`
(see file_io.cpp:82-85). Any two threads calling `read_direct()` on the same `InputFile`
concurrently race on this member: both may observe `direct_fd_ < 0`, open two descriptors, and the
loser leaks one (or, worse, a torn `int` write leaves a bogus fd value). `pread` itself is
thread-safe, so this mutable state is the only reason the class is not safely concurrent. Either
open the O_DIRECT descriptor eagerly in the constructor, guard the lazy open with a
`std::once_flag`/mutex, or explicitly document that `InputFile` is not thread-safe and must be
externally synchronized.

+     std::once_flag direct_open_once_;
      mutable int direct_fd_ = -1;


─── src/artifact/file_io.h:16-17 ───
[maintainability · low] The copy operations are deleted, but no move constructor/assignment is
declared. Because the destructor is user-provided, the move operations are not implicitly generated,
so `InputFile` is neither copyable nor movable. If a move is added later without resetting
`fd_`/`direct_fd_` in the source object, `~InputFile()` will double-close the descriptors. Consider
either explicitly declaring/defining the move operations (with `fd_ = direct_fd_ = -1` on the
moved-from object) or documenting that the type is intentionally immovable and must be held by
reference/`unique_ptr`.

(`impl_->geometries[handle.index]` is written on first use) with no synchronisation. Two threads
calling `geometry()` for the same or different handles concurrently — with or without a preceding
`open_all()` — produce a data race on the `geometries` vector, which is undefined behaviour. Either
make `geometry()` race-free (e.g. precompute in the constructor, use `std::call_once`/per-element
once-flags, or protect the cache), or explicitly document that `geometry()` (unlike the read paths
after `open_all()`) is not safe for concurrent use.

`artifact/schema.h`), which is fragile and not guaranteed across standard-library implementations.
Add the direct includes.

+ #include <string>

─── src/artifact/framing.h:11-11 ───

─── src/core/tensor.h:14-15 ───
[bug · medium] The default field initializers produce an inconsistent "null/sentinel" tensor.
`Tensor{}` (used widely across the codebase, e.g. `... : Tensor{}` and `Tensor cache_keys =
quantized ? Tensor{} : ...`) ends up with `ne = {1,1,1,1}` and `nb = {0,0,0,0}` while `data ==
nullptr`. Because the parameterized constructor is the only thing that establishes the stride
invariant (`set_contiguous_strides`), every default-constructed Tensor silently violates it.
Consequences: `numel()` returns 1 and `bytes()` returns a non-zero value even though no storage is
owned, so callers cannot distinguish an empty/unset tensor from a real 1-element tensor; and if a
default-constructed tensor's `ne` is later populated without recomputing `nb`, all strides stay 0,
leading to out-of-bounds indexing. Consider giving the sentinel an explicit, self-consistent
representation (and a validity predicate) instead of relying on `data == nullptr`.



─── src/core/tensor.h:12-12 ───
[maintainability · medium] `data` is a raw, non-owning `void*` and its lifetime/aliasing semantics
are undocumented. `slice`/`permute`/`view` copy this pointer, so all derived tensors alias the same
buffer with no ownership tracking; a copied Tensor can easily outlive the buffer it points at. Also,
accepting `void*` silently drops const-ness, so a Tensor can be constructed from a read-only buffer
and later written through. Please document the ownership/lifetime contract (non-owning view, caller
must outlive) and consider a `const void*` overload or a validated accessor to prevent accidental
mutation of read-only memory.



─── src/core/tensor.h:26-27 ───
[style · low] Minor type inconsistency across the API: `slice(int dim, ...)` and
`permute(std::initializer_list<int>)` use plain `int`, whereas the shapes use `std::int32_t` (`ne`,
constructor, `view`, `reshape`). Using `int32_t` consistently would avoid platform-dependent
narrowing surprises and keep the interface uniform.



─── src/core/weight_view.h:15-15 ───
[bug · medium] `as_dense` maps every non-FP32 `QType` to `DType::BF16`, including `QType::INT32`
(and any quantized format). This silently reinterprets the payload with the wrong element type:
`weight_view.cpp::direct_dtype` maps `INT32 -> DType::I32`, and the quantized layouts (`NVFP4`,
`Q4_G64_FP16`, `FP8_E4M3FN_ROW_BF16`, ...) have entirely different bit packings. Since this is a
header-level helper many files include, a stray non-BF16/FP32 `Weight` yields a valid-looking
`Tensor` over garbage. Consider rejecting unexpected formats (e.g. mirror `direct_dtype` /
`word_bytes`) or asserting BF16/FP32 rather than defaulting to BF16.



─── src/core/weight_view.h:24-26 ───
[bug · low] The `default:` branch assumes rank 4 and reads `shape[3]`, but it also catches `ndim ==
0` (the default value of `Weight::ndim`) and any `ndim > 4`. A default-constructed or malformed
`Weight` is therefore silently turned into a `1x1x1x1` tensor instead of being reported, which can
mask caller bugs. Handle the invalid ranks explicitly (throw/assert) instead of folding them into
the 4-D case.



─── src/core/weight_view.h:16-16 ───
[maintainability · low] `const_cast` strips the constness of `weight.qdata` so a const `Weight` can
be exposed as a mutable `Tensor`. Callers receive a handle that can write through to the original
const-decoded weight bytes, defeating the const contract. If `Tensor`'s read path does not need
mutability, prefer a const-correct overload/factory; otherwise document that the returned view must
be treated as read-only.



─── src/artifact/file_io.cpp:82-85 ───
[bug · medium] `read_direct` is a `const` method that lazily opens `direct_fd_` (declared `mutable`
in the header). If two threads call `read_direct` on the same `InputFile` concurrently, they both
observe `direct_fd_ < 0`, both call `open`, and one of the resulting descriptors is leaked forever
(the member can only hold one value); the unsynchronized read/write of `direct_fd_` is also a data
race (UB). Since the API is `const` and is intended for large payload reads, it invites concurrent
use. Please either open `direct_fd_` eagerly in the constructor, or guard the lazy initialization
(e.g. `std::once_flag`/`std::call_once` or an atomic CAS loop), and document the thread-safety
contract.



─── src/artifact/file_io.cpp:76-80 ───
[bug · low] Unlike `read_exact`, `read_direct` never validates that `offset` is within the file
(`offset <= bytes_`) nor that `offset + destination.size()` stays in range. A caller passing an
out-of-range offset gets a silent short read (possibly 0) instead of a clear `ArtifactError`, which
can mask logic errors upstream. Consider mirroring the bounds check used in `read_exact` (a short
read due to a legitimate final block is still allowed by returning the actual byte count).



─── src/artifact/formats.h:11-12 ───
[maintainability · low] `format_name`/`layout_name` are `noexcept` and the implementation in

─── src/artifact/formats.h:9-10 ───

─── src/artifact/views.cpp:14-16 ───
[maintainability · medium] This `Residency::HostFile` guard is a per-reference invariant, but it is
placed inside the per-part loop. Consequently it is re-evaluated on every iteration (redundant) and

─── src/core/nvtx.h:239-239 ───

─── src/core/nvtx.h:258-259 ───

─── src/core/decode_graph.h:39-39 ───

─── src/core/decode_graph.h:28-28 ───
[maintainability · low] These wrappers hold mutable CUDA handles (`graph_` / `exec_`) and expose

─── src/artifact/schema.h:104-104 ───

─── src/artifact/schema.h:123-123 ───

─── src/core/checked_math.h:20-22 ───
[bug · medium] `aligned_to` documents the "power of two" precondition but never enforces it, and
unlike `align_up` (which validates and throws) it silently returns a wrong answer for invalid input:
with `alignment == 0` the mask becomes all-ones so every non-null pointer reports *not* aligned, and
with a non-power-of-two such as 3 only bit 1 is tested, so misaligned pointers are reported as
aligned. Since all callers use the result to decide whether a buffer is safe to hand to a kernel, a
silent false-positive can lead to misaligned device accesses. Consider asserting the precondition
(debug builds) and/or returning an explicit failure for `alignment == 0`.

+ // Add #include <cassert> for the debug-only check.
  [[nodiscard]] inline bool aligned_to(const void* pointer, std::uintptr_t alignment) noexcept {
+     // Precondition: `alignment` is a non-zero power of two.
+     assert(alignment != 0 && (alignment & (alignment - 1)) == 0);
      return pointer != nullptr && (reinterpret_cast<std::uintptr_t>(pointer) & (alignment - 1)) == 0;
  }


─── src/core/checked_math.h:42-43 ───
[test · low] This header carries the project's shared overflow/alignment helpers but has no unit
tests (no `tests/` file references `core/checked_math.h`). The boundary cases that are easy to get
wrong here are exactly the ones worth pinning down: `checked_add`/`checked_mul` at
`std::numeric_limits<T>::max()` (and the `checked_mul` `a == 0` path that must not divide by zero),
narrow types smaller than `int` where integral promotion applies (e.g.
`std::uint8_t`/`std::uint16_t`), `align_up` with `alignment == 1`/`0`/non-power-of-two, and
`align_up` where `value + alignment - 1` overflows `T`. Please add a dedicated test target covering
these.



─── src/core/arena.h:74-75 ───

─── src/core/arena.h:77-77 ───
[bug · medium] `DeviceArena` mutates shared state (`off_`, `peak_`) unconditionally and the `Scope`

`alloc_bytes`/`alloc`/`reset`/scope destruction on the same arena is a data race that can hand out
overlapping regions or corrupt `peak_`. If the arena is expected to be single-owner/single-threaded,
state that explicitly in the class comment (or assert it); otherwise the mutation points must be
synchronized.



─── src/core/arena.h:60-60 ───
[bug · medium] `Scope` holds a raw `DeviceArena*`, yet `DeviceArena` is movable (and is stored as

the scope's rewind targets the moved-from arena and never restores the new arena's `off_` (silent
workspace growth); if the arena is destroyed first, `~Scope()` dereferences a dangling pointer (UB).
Either delete/prevent `DeviceArena` moves while scopes exist or document that a Scope must not
outlive the arena object it was created from.



─── src/core/startup.h:22-22 ───
[bug · medium] The observer is captured by address from a `const StartupObserver&` parameter and

─── src/core/startup.h:46-47 ───
[bug · medium] The two branches are inconsistent: `total_` is only overwritten when the argument is
non-zero (so `total_` can never be reset to 0), while `current_` is overwritten unconditionally. As
a result, calling `complete()` with the default arguments after `progress(copied, total)` publishes
a terminal `Complete` event whose `current` is 0 rather than the last reported value. This is
currently masked only because `startup_log.cpp` falls back to `event.total` when `current == 0`; any
other observer reading `event.current` gets a misleading value. Consider defaulting `current` to
`total_` (or making the parameters mandatory) and applying the same `total` rule in
`progress()`/`complete()`.



─── src/core/startup.h:38-38 ───
[maintainability · low] `progress()`/`complete()` and the destructor all mutate `current_`, `total_`
and `terminal_` with no synchronization. If a phase ever reports progress from multiple threads
(e.g. a parallel materialization loop), these are data races. If single-threaded use is a hard

─── src/core/startup.h:56-57 ───
[bug · low] For `Begin` and `Progress` events `elapsed_ns` is forced to 0, so intermediate observers

─── src/core/host_worker_pool.cpp:107-107 ───
[performance · medium] `wait_for` with a fixed 10 ms timeout is a polling workaround for a
lost-wakeup that cannot actually happen here: `queue` is only modified while `mutex` is held (the
pop in `worker_loop` happens under the lock, and the `queue_space` notify follows the unlock), so
any producer that re-checks `queue.size()` after the pop is guaranteed to observe the freed slot via
the mutex happens-before edge. Waiting without a predicate therefore adds up to 10 ms of latency per
enqueue under backpressure and wastes wakeups. Use a predicate-based `wait` so producers are woken
exactly when a slot is freed.

-         impl_->queue_space.wait_for(lock, std::chrono::milliseconds(10));
+         impl_->queue_space.wait(lock, [this] {
+             return impl_->stopping || impl_->queue.size() < impl_->queue_capacity;
+         });


─── src/core/host_worker_pool.cpp:66-70 ───
[bug · medium] `std::packaged_task::operator()` does not only rethrow the wrapped callable's
exceptions (those are captured in the shared state) — it can itself throw `std::future_error` (e.g.
`no_state` / `promise_already_satisfied`). Treating every escaping exception as a fatal contract
violation and calling `std::terminate()` will therefore kill the whole process with no diagnostic
for an otherwise recoverable condition. At minimum log/report the exception before terminating, or
degrade gracefully instead of an unconditional `std::terminate()`.



─── src/core/host_worker_pool.cpp:73-73 ───
[maintainability · low] This check can never be true: this worker incremented `active` itself before
running the task, and only the owning worker decrements it, so `active` is guaranteed to be >= 1 at
this point. The branch is effectively dead defensive code; if the intent is an invariant assertion,
that should be explicit (e.g. an assert) rather than an unreachable `std::terminate()`.



─── src/core/weight.h:68-69 ───
[documentation · low] The comment documents the divisor index as `r / weight_divisor_rows`, i.e.
consumers divide by this field, but the field has no stated invariant and defaults to 0. A plane
whose `weight_divisors` is set while `weight_divisor_rows` was never populated (or a
default-constructed `Weight`) makes that integer division undefined. The NVFP4 validator
(`validate_nvfp4_weight`) enforces `rows > 0`, but nothing in this header expresses the rule, and
not every reader of `weight_divisors` goes through that validator. Please document the invariant
(`weight_divisor_rows > 0` whenever `weight_divisors != nullptr`) and the divisor element type
(float) — the comment only calls them "divisors" while consumers cast them to `const float*`.



─── src/core/weight.h:38-40 ───
[maintainability · low] The defaults are inconsistent: `ndim` defaults to 0 ("no dimensions") while
`shape`/`padded_shape` default to `{1,1,1,1}`, which looks like a valid 4-D tensor. Consumers that
branch on `ndim` (e.g. `as_dense` in weight_view.h) hit their `default:`/4-D arm for the 0 sentinel,
so a default-initialized `Weight` is silently interpreted as 4-D rather than rejected as invalid.
Consider making the sentinel unambiguous (e.g. keep the shape entries at the "unset" value or treat
`ndim == 0` explicitly) so a default aggregate cannot be mistaken for a real 4-D weight.

─── src/core/kv_page_ref.h:38-39 ───
[bug · medium] The `HostPageUnit` constructor does not enforce the documented encodable range (`unit
<= kMaxHostPageUnit == 0x7fffffff`). For any `unit > 0x7fffffff`, `~unit` is non-negative, so the
resulting `word_` is silently reinterpreted as a *Device* page group by `host()`,
`paged_kv_read_page` (`word >= 0`), etc. This is a silent aliasing bug rather than an obvious
failure (e.g. `HostPageUnit{kMaxHostPageUnit + 1U}` yields `word_ == 0`). Since `kMaxHostPageUnit`
already exists to document the limit, consider asserting the precondition (or clamping) so misuse is
caught instead of producing a valid-looking Device word. Note the boundary casts in callers such as
`decoder_state.cpp` (`static_cast<HostPageUnit>(record * record_units)`) can exceed this limit on
overflow, so a guard here is valuable.



─── src/core/kv_page_ref.h:57-57 ───
[maintainability · low] Every other member of `KVPageRef` is annotated `NINFER_KV_REF_HD`, but this
defaulted `operator==` is not. As a result it is host-only and cannot be used to compare refs inside
`__device__` kernels (e.g. in the paged-ops resolution code), even though the type is explicitly
designed for device use — callers must fall back to comparing `.word()`. Consider providing it as an
`NINFER_KV_REF_HD` function so equality is usable on both arms, consistent with the rest of the

─── src/core/paged_kv_storage.h:23-26 ───
[bug · low] `data_leading_extent`/`scale_leading_extent` are signed `std::int32_t` but are cast to

that can be constructed directly (its default is 0, and nothing prevents a caller from assigning a
negative extent), a negative value would wrap to a huge `size_t` and silently produce a bogus
(enormous) byte count that then drives allocations/workspace sizing. Consider making the extents
unsigned, or validating them (e.g. throw when negative) before the conversion.



─── src/core/paged_kv_storage.h:72-72 ───
[maintainability · low] The plane extents are hardcoded magic numbers (`256`, `128`) while the guard

silently desync and the layout would no longer match the codec geometry. Derive the extents from
`head_dim` instead (e.g. `head_dim` for the full-width codecs and `head_dim / 2` for the NVFP4
packed plane). This applies to the Int8Group64, Fp8E4M3Row256, Nvfp4Group16 and Fp8KeyNvfp4Value
branches.

─── src/core/paged_kv_cache.h:288-289 ───
[bug · medium] `DeviceKVPagePool` keeps a scratch duplicate-detection cache (`validation_marks_`
plus the `validation_stamp_` counter) as `mutable` members, so the `const` methods that call
`validate_distinct_pages` (e.g. `zero_pages`, `copy_from_host`) mutate shared pool state. Two
concurrent `const` calls on the same pool (e.g. one zero/copy per CUDA stream/thread) will
read/write `validation_stamp_` and `validation_marks_` without synchronization, which is a data race
(UB) and can also produce spurious `duplicate_message` throws. Either make this scratch state
per-call/thread-local, or explicitly document that a `DeviceKVPagePool` (and everything reachable
from it, including `KVExecutionTablePool::publish`) is single-threaded-only.



─── src/core/paged_kv_cache.h:195-196 ───

ownership, while `release()` drops ownership as well. Their names do not convey this difference, and
`release()` returning `void` is inconsistent with
`DeviceKVPageLease::release()`/`KVExecutionRowLease::release()` which return `bool`. Please document
the contracts (and consider aligning the return type) so callers pick the right one.



─── src/core/paged_kv_cache.h:134-137 ───

─── src/core/device.h:90-90 ───
[bug · medium] `CudaCompletionEvent` captures the owning device id but no member function ever
exposes or uses it: `record`/`wait`/`ready`/`synchronize` neither take a device nor call
`cudaSetDevice`, and the implementation never reads `device_`. Because CUDA event operations bind to
the calling thread's *current* device, using this event from a worker thread whose current device
differs from the creating device will fail (invalid resource handle) or silently target the wrong
device. Either bind `device_` (e.g. `cudaSetDevice(device_)`) at the start of
`record`/`wait`/`ready`/`synchronize`, or drop the unused member to avoid implying device affinity
that isn't enforced.

─── src/core/host_worker_pool.h:29-30 ───
[maintainability · medium] Rule-of-five inconsistency: `~HostWorkerPool()` is user-declared (needed
because `Impl` is incomplete in the header), which suppresses the implicit move
constructor/assignment, while copy operations are explicitly deleted. The net effect is that the
class is silently neither copyable nor movable. This is easy to trip over later (e.g. a factory
returning `HostWorkerPool` by value, or storing it by value in a container) and the failure surfaces
as a confusing compile error far from this declaration. Since the copy operations are already
spelled out, state the move intent explicitly.

─── src/core/host_worker_pool.h:32-34 ───
[documentation · medium] `submit` has two behaviors that are not reflected in its signature and are
easy for callers to get wrong: (1) it does not only report failures through the returned
`std::future` — when the pool is stopping, the underlying `enqueue` throws `std::runtime_error`
synchronously, so callers must handle both the future and a synchronous exception; and (2) `enqueue`
blocks (with periodic `queue_space` waits) while the queue is at capacity, so `submit` can block for
an unbounded time. Because of (2), calling `submit` from within a pool worker (nested submission)
can deadlock once every worker is blocked on a full queue. Please document this contract
(throw-on-stopping and blocking / no re-entrant submission) either in the class comment or on
`submit`.

it); the only usage is `std::chrono::milliseconds` in host_worker_pool.cpp, which relies on this
transitive include. Prefer including `<chrono>` directly in the .cpp and dropping it here, so the
header does not advertise a dependency it does not have.



─── src/artifact/reader.cpp:108-109 ───

─── src/core/decode_graph.cpp:76-77 ───
[maintainability · low] `CUDA_CHECK(expr)` stringizes `expr` in its abort message (see `cuda_check`

─── src/core/host_kv_arena.h:57-59 ───
[maintainability · low] This `valid()` only means "bound to an owner", not "the allocation is still
live". A handle copied from `HostKVAllocation::handle()` keeps reporting `valid() == true` after the
allocation has been released/regenerated, whereas `HostKVAllocationView::valid()` (and
`ConstView::valid()`) do check liveness against the arena. `HostKVAllocation::valid()` and
`HostKVAllocationRecipe::valid()` share the same weak check, so the same name has two different
meanings across these types. Callers trusting the name can pass a stale handle. Consider renaming

─── src/core/linear_attention_state.cpp:164-164 ───
[performance · medium] `slot_view()` re-runs the entire `all_layers_view()` validation on every
call: it walks all layers, reconstructs an `expected` Tensor per layer and re-derives both layer
strides. On top of that, `conv_slot(0, slot)` / `recurrent_slot(0, slot)` re-run
`validate_layer_slot` and `std::vector::at`. The inventory and the layer pitches are immutable after
construction, but this accessor is (per its consumers, e.g. `StateImageDevicePool::slot_view`) a
per-slot query. Consider computing the two layer strides once in the constructor (they are already
validated there via `(void)all_layers_view();`) and storing them, so this becomes O(1); at minimum,
call `layer_stride_bytes(conv_, "conv")` / `layer_stride_bytes(recurrent_, "recurrent")` directly
here instead of the full `all_layers_view()` (mirroring `CyclicKVCache::slot_view`, which uses the
lightweight `layer_pitch`).



─── src/core/linear_attention_state.cpp:123-123 ───
[maintainability · medium] The conv/recurrent `Tensor` geometry is spelled out in three independent
places — `plan_linear_attention_state_pool`, this constructor, and `validate_state_tensor`. If the
two never drift it is fine, but any edit to one of the shapes (e.g. reordering dimensions with an
equal element count) has to be mirrored in the other sites, and the `bytes()` comparisons only catch
size changes, not dimension order. Extract a small helper (e.g. `make_conv_shape(dtype, spec)` /
`make_recurrent_shape(spec)`) and reuse it in all three sites to keep a single source of truth.



─── src/core/linear_attention_state.cpp:193-194 ───
[maintainability · low] The two components' strides are validated in isolation, so the combined
(interleaved) geometry is never checked: `layer_stride_bytes(conv_, ...)` only asserts `stride >=
conv_layer_bytes`, and neither check detects a conv layer region overlapping the recurrent region of
the same or the next layer, nor that the conv and recurrent layer strides agree. As these two
strides are the sole guarantee backing `all_layers_view()`/`slot_view()` (which downstream code uses
to walk layers with raw pointer arithmetic), consider validating the interleaved layer spans
together (e.g. a combined stride check over both components).

─── src/core/cyclic_kv_cache.cpp:15-16 ───
[bug · low] `align_up_u32` silently assumes `alignment` is a non-zero power of two: the mask trick
`(value + alignment - 1) & ~(alignment - 1)` yields wrong results for any other alignment, and
`alignment == 0` underflows the mask. `ninfer::align_up` (layout.cpp) explicitly validates this
precondition and throws, so this local re-implementation is strictly weaker. In addition, the
overflow case is reported as `std::invalid_argument`, while equivalent overflow guards in this
codebase (`padded_dflash_capacity`, `plan_linear_attention_state_pool`, `Tensor::bytes`) throw
`std::overflow_error` — since `plan_cyclic_kv_cache` runs before `plan_host_state_image`, the same
bad capacity is classified differently depending on which planner throws first. Validate the
alignment and use `std::overflow_error` for the range failure.



─── src/artifact/materializer.cpp:96-96 ───
[bug · medium] `geometry.divisor_count` is not validated here. If it is 0, `words` is empty and the
`read_u32_le(words.data())` at the end of this function dereferences an empty buffer (UB/crash).
Additionally, `host` is never checked to cover `[divisor_offset, divisor_offset + bytes)`, so an
inconsistent geometry silently causes an out-of-bounds heap read on the retained host buffer.
Suggest rejecting a zero divisor count and bounds-checking the region inside `host` before copying.



─── src/artifact/materializer.cpp:282-282 ───
[maintainability · low] `object.bytes` is a `std::uint64_t`, so comparing it against
`std::numeric_limits<std::size_t>::max()` is always false on the 64-bit targets this code runs on —
the second half of the condition is dead code (and typically triggers a tautological-compare
warning). The same always-false pattern appears in the device-capacity guard
(`plan.device_capacity_bytes > std::numeric_limits<std::size_t>::max()`). If the goal is to guard a
narrowing `size_t` conversion, use a portable check such as `std::cmp_greater(object.bytes,
std::numeric_limits<std::size_t>::max())`.

─── src/core/paged_kv_cache.cpp:830-833 ───
[bug · medium] Liveness-validation gap compared with the other `publish` overloads. The
`DeviceKVPageHandle`/`DeviceKVPageLease` overloads resolve the page through
`pages_->physical_index(...)`, which validates pool ownership, allocation state and generation. This
overload only range-checks the Device word (`word() < capacity_pages()`), so a word that names a
currently-free/stale page is copied verbatim into the block table. If the caller reuses a KVPageRef
after the page was dematerialized, the attention kernel will index a device page no longer owned by
that sequence (OOB read/write, silent corruption). Suggest exposing an allocation query on
`DeviceKVPagePool` and rejecting unallocated Device words here (or explicitly document that
KVPageRef device words are trusted and unchecked).



─── src/core/paged_kv_cache.cpp:582-582 ───
[bug · low] Asymmetric validation: `copy_from_host` and `zero_pages` both call
`validate_distinct_pages`, but `copy_to_host` only verifies that each source handle is live and
never that the source pages are distinct. Duplicate entries silently produce an ambiguous D2H
transfer (the same device page is written into several host slots) that no caller-side check can
detect. Call `validate_distinct_pages(source, "Paged KV D2H source contains duplicate pages")` for
consistency and safety.



─── src/core/paged_kv_cache.cpp:246-248 ───
[bug · low] Unsigned wrap on invariant violation: if the accounting invariant (`allocated + reserved
<= capacity`) is ever broken, this subtraction wraps to a huge value instead of failing fast, so
`reserve()` will hand out far more pages than the pool physically owns; the error then surfaces much
later as an unrelated `std::logic_error`/OOB. Consider asserting the invariant here (or clamping
with a debug check) so the violation is reported at its source.

─── src/core/weight_view.cpp:383-387 ───
[bug · medium] The `RowGroup` layout (`QType::Q4_G32_FP16_ROWS`) never assigns `out.scale_dtype`, so
it keeps the struct default `DType::FP32`, unlike every other quantized branch (RowSplit=FP16,
RowScale=BF16, BlockScale=FP8_E4M3FN). The scale plane of this format is binary16 — as the
`RowGroup` branch of `weight_geometry` documents ("one binary16 scale each") and as the `k / 32 * 2`
scale row stride used later implies — so a consumer that inspects or compares the scale dtype (e.g.
the gemma4 expert-bank consistency check `member.scale_dtype != first.scale_dtype`, or any RowGroup
op requiring FP16) will see FP32 and mis-type the plane. Add an explicit RowGroup branch.

─── src/core/weight_view.cpp:344-345 ───
[bug · low] `out.input_scales` points at the EXL3 `suh` plane, which holds one `suh[k]` per divisor
(i.e. `4 * k` bytes when `divisor_count == 1`; `svh` is the separate plane at
`output_scale_offset`). The `scale_ne`/`scale_nb` values written here describe an `[n, k]` FP32
plane occupying `n * 4 + k * 4` bytes, so the declared extent overruns the plane whenever `n > 1`,
and the strides are not self-consistent under any single convention (`nb[2] == k * 4`, not `nb[1] *
ne[1]`). Compare the RowSplit branch above, which describes its plane as `{groups, n}` with
Fortran-order strides; this should describe `suh` as `{k, divisor_count}` (or leave
`scale_ne`/`scale_nb` unset).



─── src/core/host_kv_arena.cpp:562-564 ───
[bug · medium] `apply_recipe` aborts the whole process on any internal inconsistency, which
contradicts the documented contract of this API (the header states that a `false` return leaves the
arena and every input allocation unchanged — nothing documents a fatal path).

The per-target validation only checks the *count* and that the outputs are empty; it never checks
that each output slot actually corresponds to the planned target (there is no way for the caller to
know the required ordering from the interface). If a caller supplies the outputs in a different
order than the requests were planned with, `allocate` takes a different first-fit run than the one
recorded in the recipe and this line kills the process instead of returning `false`. The same
applies to the earlier `if (!allocation->release()) { std::terminate(); }` in the release loop.

Either (a) validate the predicted slots before the first mutation (e.g. re-simulate the free lists
as `plan_after_releases` does, or verify the whole request/order mapping up front) and return
`false`, or (b) at minimum document explicitly that an inconsistent recipe/output ordering
terminates.



─── src/core/host_kv_arena.cpp:205-206 ───
[maintainability · low] `HostKVAllocationView` and `HostKVAllocationConstView` duplicate the whole
addressing/validation logic (`valid`, `layout`, `subview`, `plane_page`, `contiguous_pages`)
verbatim — only the constness of `region_`/return type differs. Any future change to the
slot->address formula (division by `chunk_pages_`, `layout_->layer_of(plane)`, `layer_span` pitch)
has to be kept in sync in two places, and the mutable/const implementations of `subview` are already
written differently (fresh construction vs. copy-and-adjust), which makes divergence likely.
Consider having the const view delegate to a shared private helper (or derive the const view from
the mutable one, converting the pointer) so the arithmetic lives in a single place.

─── src/models/gemma4/vision.h:33-33 ───
[bug · medium] `patches()` multiplies two `std::int32_t` grid dimensions directly into an

a negative or unexpectedly small value, silently corrupting every downstream size derivation
(`vector` sizing, `cudaMemcpy` lengths, workspace math in `encode_image`) and potentially defeating
the range checks that are meant to run after this call. Since the struct itself imposes no bound on
the dimensions, compute the product in a wider type and/or validate that the result fits before
returning.

─── src/models/qwen3_5/config.h:113-113 ───
[bug · high] `heads()` returns `(ngram_size - 1) * heads_per_ngram`, so it evaluates to 0 whenever
`heads_per_ngram == 0` (or `ngram_size == 1`). Nothing in the header or the parser rejects a zero

`uint32_t` is UB (typically SIGFPE), so a malformed artifact config crashes the loader. Guard the
divisor (or assert `heads() != 0` via a validated invariant).

-     [[nodiscard]] std::uint32_t row_width() const noexcept { return embed_dim / heads(); }

+         const auto h = heads();
+         return h == 0 ? 0U : embed_dim / h;
+     }

transitively, bad `row_width()`/table sizes). Use a guarded subtraction.

      [[nodiscard]] std::uint32_t heads() const noexcept {

+         return ngram_size == 0 ? 0U : (ngram_size - 1) * heads_per_ngram;

─── src/models/qwen3_5/execution/ple_rows.h:33-36 ───
[documentation · medium] The zero-argument `data(range)` / `read()` contract is under-specified for
a mutable, reusable buffer: pointers returned by `data()` are invalidated by the next `read()`
(which may re-`std::free`/reallocate `staging_`) and `read()` mutates `pages_`/`located_`/`staging_`
without any synchronization, so it must not run concurrently with `data()`. Please document these
lifetime/thread-safety guarantees on the methods; a caller caching a `data()` pointer across a
`read()` (or calling `read()` from another thread while decoding) would silently read freed/aliased
memory.

+     // until the next read() on this reader (or its destruction).
      void read(std::span<const Range> ranges);
  
+     // Addresses range i of the most recent read(); the pointer is invalidated by the next read().
      [[nodiscard]] const std::byte* data(std::size_t range) const { return located_.at(range); }


─── src/models/qwen3_5/execution/ple_rows.h:60-60 ───
[maintainability · low] `staging_` is a raw owning pointer freed manually with `std::free` in the

with a free-deleter (or a small RAII type) would remove the manual lifetime management and make the
class move-correct/safe against future edits (the user-declared destructor currently suppresses
implicit move, leaving the type immovable).

-     std::byte* staging_        = nullptr;

─── src/models/gemma4/cache.cpp:79-80 ───
[bug · high] Device-memory leak on reconfigure. `std::vector<void*>().swap(owned_)` only clears the
ownership list (a `std::vector<void*>` does not free its pointees); every pointer previously
returned by `cudaMalloc` in `ensure` is dropped without `cudaFree`. If `configure` is ever called on
a cache that was already allocated (a public, documented entry point), all per-layer device buffers
leak, and `bytes_` is then reset to 0 so the loss is invisible. Free the owned pointers before
discarding them, as the destructor and move-assignment already do.

Suggested:
```cpp
for (void* pointer : owned_) {
    if (pointer != nullptr) (void)cudaFree(pointer);
}
owned_.clear();
bytes_ = 0;
```



─── src/models/gemma4/cache.cpp:250-253 ───
[bug · medium] Missing kind validation on `keys()`/`values()`. The header contract states a
quantized sliding layer "has no BF16 rows and refuses these", but neither overload enforces it: for
a quantized layer `ensure` never assigns `entry.keys`/`entry.values`, so this non-const accessor
(and the const one via `existing(..., allocated)`, which passes because `allocated == true`)
silently returns a default/null `Tensor` instead of throwing. A caller that trusts the contract
would then use a null-data tensor. The accessors should reject quantized layers the same way
`key_codes()`/`value_codes()` reject non-quantized ones, e.g. add `if (quantized(layer)) throw
std::logic_error("KvCache: the layer's ring is quantized");` after `ensure(layer)` (and in the const
overloads).

─── src/models/qwen3_5/execution/ple_gather.h:33-34 ───

─── src/models/qwen3_5/execution/probe_registry.h:17-17 ───
[bug · high] The registry keeps `qdata` as a raw pointer into the model's resident weight store (for
EXL3 `out.qdata = region.parent->data`, see `core/weight_view.cpp`), but `probe_targets()` is a
process-global static that is never cleared. Nothing resets it between preparations, so (a) after a
`Model` is destroyed the stored pointers dangle, and the M5 probe would then read freed memory, and
(b) a later preparation whose newly allocated resident buffer happens to reuse a stale address is
silently skipped by the `qdata` comparison in `register_probe_target`, so the target is missing
entirely. Add an explicit reset (e.g. `clear_probe_targets()` called at the start of `Parameters`
construction) so the registry's lifetime is bound to one preparation.



─── src/models/qwen3_5/execution/probe_registry.h:22-25 ───
[maintainability · medium] This accessor hands out a mutable reference to the process-global vector,
so any caller can clear or otherwise mutate the registry without going through
`register_probe_target`, and the invariant maintained by the dedup loop can be broken. Prefer
exposing a const view (`const std::vector<ProbeTarget>& probe_targets()`) plus dedicated
mutation/reset helpers.

Additionally, this mutable global is mutated with no synchronization: the function-local static
initialization is thread-safe, but the read-for-dedup followed by `push_back` is not. Preparation is
currently sequential, but nothing here states that requirement (compare `qwen4_expert_pager.h`,
which documents its single-thread constraint). Either document that only the preparation thread may
call this, or guard it with a mutex.



─── src/models/qwen3_5/execution/probe_registry.h:34-34 ───
[bug · medium] Deduplication is by `qdata` pointer only, while the header documents targets as
"keyed by its logical name". Those two keys disagree: a paired projection (e.g. QKV / gate-up) is

the same `name` — so two entries can share an identical `name`. A consumer that identifies a target
to perturb by name cannot distinguish them and will be ambiguous. Either enforce/validate name
uniqueness here, store the projection role alongside the name, or update the header contract to

─── src/models/gemma4/config.h:76-78 ───
[bug · low] Both `sliding_attention` and `geometry` index `layer_types` with `.at()`, so they throw

the parse functions advertise, and it makes these `[[nodiscard]]` predicate-style accessors
unexpectedly throwing — which is hazardous for the `noexcept` call sites that use them. Consider
documenting the precondition explicitly (or asserting / returning a safe default) instead of relying
on a throwing container accessor.

─── src/models/qwen3_5/execution/ple_rows.cpp:140-140 ───
[bug · medium] If one worker fails (e.g. an I/O error), `task.get()` rethrows immediately and the
remaining futures in `pending` are simply destroyed without waiting. The still-queued/running tasks
keep a captured `this` and write into `staging_`/`pages_`; the pool only drains them when it is
destroyed or when its queue is next serviced. If the caller catches the exception and issues another
`read()` on the same reader, `reserve()` may free and reallocate `staging_` while those stale tasks
are still writing -> use-after-free / heap corruption. Await every future before propagating
(collect the first exception, then rethrow after the loop).

-     for (auto& task : pending) { task.get(); }
+     std::exception_ptr failure;
+     for (auto& task : pending) {
+         try {
+             task.get();
+         } catch (...) {
+             if (!failure) { failure = std::current_exception(); }
+         }
+     }
+     if (failure) { std::rethrow_exception(failure); }


─── src/models/qwen3_5/execution/ple_rows.cpp:104-105 ───
[maintainability · low] The old staging buffer is released before the new one is obtained, so a
failed allocation (or the `bad_alloc` thrown below) leaves a partially destroyed state, and the
previously read pages are lost even though they could still have been served for a smaller request.
It is also manual resource management for a uniquely-owned buffer. Allocate first and only then
release the old block, and prefer holding the buffer in an owning smart pointer with an aligned
deleter



─── src/models/qwen3_5/execution/ple_rows.cpp:53-54 ───
[bug · low] A `pread` returning 0 is treated as end-of-file and the remainder of the page is
silently zero-filled. This also triggers for a page in the middle of the volume whose data has gone
missing (truncated/corrupt volume), so invalid n-gram codes are produced with no error at all.
Consider validating the page against the file size (obtained once via `fstat`) and rejecting reads
that fall inside the declared volume but return fewer bytes than expected, instead of masking them.



─── src/models/qwen3_5/execution/ple_gather.cpp:64-64 ───

performs `embed_dim / 0` — undefined behavior/crash — before the body's `config.ngram_size < 2`

─── src/models/gemma4/program.cpp:192-194 ───

─── src/models/qwen3_5/execution/gdn.cpp:43-45 ───
[maintainability · low] `gdn_snapshot_workspace_bytes` and `gdn_record_workspace_bytes` are
byte-for-byte identical apart from the capacity function they call; the branch predicate (`single &&
single->weight.qtype != QType::Q8_G32_FP16`) encodes two different decisions at once — "is the
projection a single parent" and "is it Q8" — with the paired case silently falling into the
config-based `else` even though `single == nullptr` there. Because the two bodies must stay in
lockstep (a future change to the routing or to the width arguments applied to one but not the other
would be easy to miss), consider extracting a small shared helper (e.g.
`projection_shape(parameters, config)` returning the qtype/shape or a small struct) and letting both
entry points call the corresponding capacity function with it. Also, unlike
`gdn_projection_workspace_bytes`, neither function validates `batch` / the width interval locally;
that is currently delegated to the downstream capacity calls, which is asymmetric and easy to break
if a new route is added.

─── src/models/qwen3_5/execution/qwen4_expert_pager.cpp:31-32 ───
[bug · medium] Resource leak if any CUDA_CHECK after this point throws: this class manages raw CUDA
resources (pinned host buffer, stream, 6 events), and the destructor is not invoked for an object
whose constructor throws. If `cudaStreamCreateWithFlags`, any `cudaEventCreateWithFlags`, or a later
check fails, `resident_` (pinned memory) plus the already-created stream/events leak.

Suggested fix: make the members RAII-owned (e.g. `std::unique_ptr<std::int32_t,
decltype(&cudaFreeHost)>` for the host buffer and stream/event wrappers with
`cudaStreamDestroy`/`cudaEventDestroy` deleters), or wrap the body after this allocation in a
try/catch that destroys the already-acquired resources before rethrowing.



─── src/models/qwen3_5/execution/qwen4_expert_pager.cpp:101-103 ───

planned short) makes `resident[...]` read out of bounds. This is a silent OOB read that then steers
the Host-copy loop.

─── src/models/qwen3_5/execution/qwen4_expert_pager.cpp:84-84 ───
[performance · low] This full-stream synchronization also waits for every other operation already
enqueued on the pager stream (for example a cache-route `expert_cache_fetch` from a previous
forward, or a fill that does not touch `resident_`), even though only preceding fills read
`resident_`. Since this runs once per staged forward, it can stall the pipeline more than necessary.

Suggestion: record an event after the fill that last consumed `resident_` and wait only on that
event, so unrelated in-flight work on `stream_` is not forced to complete. (The sync of the compute
`stream` below it is inherent to reading the snapshot back on the host.)

─── src/models/gemma4/forward.cpp:839-840 ───
[bug · medium] The caller's buffer is re-viewed as `BF16 [h, tokens]` without any check of its real
shape, dtype or contiguity: a caller that hands over a buffer narrower than `tokens` columns (e.g.
the primary pass's buffer reused for a shorter draft pass) silently gives `rmsnorm` an out-of-bounds
view. The same holds for the caller-provided `logits`/`hidden_out` in `forward_draft`, which are
written for `lanes.size()` columns unchecked. Since this API's whole contract is "views over caller
storage", a cheap validation of `ne[0] >= h && ne[1] >= tokens` (and dtype) would turn a
memory-corruption class of bug into a clear exception.

─── src/models/qwen3_5/load/bindings.h:38-39 ───
[maintainability · low] `weights` is exposed as a public mutable `std::vector<PendingWeight>` while
the parallel bookkeeping map `parameters_` is private. Callers can therefore push/erase/reorder
`weights` directly (via `.weights` or the non-const `binder`/`weights` members) and desynchronize it

─── src/models/qwen3_5/frontend/digest.h:15-16 ───
[performance · low] The cancellation/progress hook is exposed as `const std::function<void()>&`,
which type-erases the callback and forces every call site to construct a `std::function` (plus a
potential heap allocation if the callable does not fit the small-buffer optimization) on each
invocation. This API is called per media part inside a loop (e.g. `processor.cpp` hashes each
`part->media.bytes`), so the erasure cost is paid repeatedly for what is essentially a cheap
predicate. Consider taking a lightweight callable instead — e.g. a template parameter /
`std::function`-free design, or a plain `void(*)(void*)` + context pair — or at minimum document why
type erasure is required here.

cancellation/health hook (it is invoked periodically during hashing and may throw). The header gives
no contract for it: how often it is called, whether it may throw, or whether it is invoked for small
inputs at all — callers cannot tell without reading the .cpp (`digest.cpp` calls it at most once per
MiB and only while full blocks remain). Please document the callback semantics (invocation cadence,
exception propagation) and consider a more intention-revealing name such as
`on_progress`/`check_cancelled`.



─── src/models/qwen3_5/frontend/tool_call_parser.h:41-41 ───
[maintainability · medium] `in_tool_region()` returns true as soon as a single trailing `<` has been
buffered (`marker_prefix_bytes_ == 1`), i.e. before it is known to be a real tool marker. Callers
use this to pick the output branch (output_session.cpp), so ordinary content that merely ends in `<`
at a chunk boundary is reported as being inside a tool region. The name overstates the guarantee;
consider renaming to something like `may_be_in_tool_region()` or documenting that it only means "a
partial/complete `<tool_call>` marker is pending".

─── src/models/qwen3_5/frontend/tool_contract.h:59-60 ───
[bug · high] `tolerant` (and `enforce_declared_names` declared below) are declaration-scoped
properties that the tool-call parser reads off the contract (`QwenToolRegionParser{...

─── src/models/qwen3_5/load.h:42-42 ───
[documentation · low] `plan_load` builds a `LoadPlan` that stores a borrowed `const Reader*` (via

─── src/models/qwen3_5/load.h:43-44 ───
[documentation · medium] `materialize_model` consumes the plan: the implementation moves

plan (`config()`, `weights()`, `resources()`, `materialization()`, `parameter()`, `uses()`)
dereferences a null `impl_` and is undefined behavior. This single-use semantics is not documented
in the header. Consider documenting that the plan is consumed/moved-from and must not be reused, or
guard the accessors (all of them, not just `materialize_model`) against a null `impl_`.

─── src/models/qwen3_5/frontend/output_session.h:77-77 ───
[maintainability · medium] The public default constructor yields a session with a null `impl_`, and
the methods declared in this header do not share a consistent contract for that empty state: most

─── src/models/qwen3_5/frontend/tokenizer.h:96-100 ───
[bug · medium] `insert` probes until it finds a slot with `result < 0`, with no bound on the number
of probes. Termination relies entirely on an implicit invariant: the table must never hold more
entries than the `rule_count` passed to `reset` (load factor kept <= 1/2), and every stored `result`
must be >= 0. Two concrete ways to violate this and hang the loader:
1. `reset(0)` yields `capacity == 1` / `mask_ == 0`; inserting two distinct keys then loops forever.
2. `BpeMergeRule` default-initializes `result = -1`, so `insert(key, BpeMergeRule{})` writes an
entry that is indistinguishable from an empty slot, again causing a non-terminating probe.
Since this is a reusable public utility (not private to the loader), consider guarding the invariant
— e.g. assert `rule.result >= 0`, and reject/`assert` when the number of stored entries would exceed
half of `capacity` — so misuse fails fast instead of hanging.

`stop_token_ids` (a `std::vector`). `decode` is called while streaming out generated tokens, so this
copy is paid repeatedly for options that are only read. Pass by const reference instead.

-     std::string decode(std::span<const int> ids, DecodeOptions options = {}) const;
+     std::string decode(std::span<const int> ids, const DecodeOptions& options = {}) const;

─── src/models/qwen3_5/execution/visual_scatter.cpp:16-16 ───
[bug · medium] The copy length is derived from the destination tensor's *total* byte size
(`destination.bytes()`), but the source host buffer (`overlap.destination_columns`) holds exactly
`overlap.size()` elements. The caller of this helper (`scatter_shifted_visual_embeddings`) only
validates `destination_indices.ne[0] == overlap.size()`; it does not reject extra non-unit
dimensions (ne[1..3] != 1). A destination like `alloc(DType::I32, {count, 2})` therefore passes that
check and this `cudaMemcpyAsync` reads `count * 2 * 4` bytes from a `count * 4` byte host vector —
an out-of-bounds host read before `ops::scatter` gets a chance to reject the shape. Derive the
length from the source element count (or assert `destination.numel() == source_count`) instead of
trusting the destination size.



─── src/models/qwen3_5/execution/visual_scatter.cpp:29-29 ───
[bug · low] `overlap.size()` (size_t) is narrowed to int32_t both for the validation comparison and
for the subsequent `slice` length. If the vector ever exceeds INT32_MAX this conversion is
implementation-defined, so the validation could spuriously pass and `count` become negative, after
the device memcpy has already executed. Prefer reusing the already-validated destination dimension
(`destination_indices.ne[0]`) as `count`, which removes the narrowing entirely.
